::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::: ltx_page_content
# TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization {#tilemaxsim-io-aware-gpu-maxsim-scoring-with-dimension-tiling-and-fused-product-quantization .ltx_title .ltx_title_document}

::: ltx_authors
[ [Ashutosh Sharma ]{.ltx_personname}[[
[[Affiliation: ]{.ltx_contact_name}[MIT-IBM Watson AI Lab]{#id1
.ltx_text .ltx_affiliation_institution}, [USA]{#id2 .ltx_text
.ltx_affiliation_country} ]{.ltx_contact .ltx_role_affiliation} [[email:
]{.ltx_contact_name}<ashutosh.sharma7@ibm.com> ]{.ltx_contact
.ltx_role_email}]{.ltx_author_notes_content}]{.ltx_author_notes}]{.ltx_creator
.ltx_role_author}
:::

::: {#abstract1 .ltx_abstract}
###### Abstract. {#abstract. .ltx_title .ltx_title_abstract}

Multi-vector retrieval models such as ColBERT achieve state-of-the-art
accuracy through fine-grained token-level MaxSim scoring, yet existing
GPU implementations leave most hardware performance on the table. We
conduct a roofline analysis of MaxSim on modern GPUs and identify a
severe *bandwidth gap*: naive implementations achieve only 5--18% of
peak HBM bandwidth because they materialize the $N_{q}\times N_{d}$
similarity matrix, wasting memory traffic on intermediate data that is
consumed once and discarded. We present [TileMaxSim]{#abstract1.1.2
.ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, a family of IO-aware Triton kernels that
close this gap through three techniques: (1) *multi-query SRAM tiling*
that streams document embeddings through shared memory while
accumulating per-query-token maxima in registers, reading each embedding
from HBM exactly once; (2) *dimension tiling* that partitions the
embedding dimension into 128-wide chunks, enabling efficient scoring for
$d>128$ embeddings that overflow shared memory (not handled by prior
multi-vector scoring kernels); and (3) *fused PQ decompression+scoring*
via shared-memory lookup tables that scores compressed documents without
explicit decompression, reducing HBM I/O by up to
$\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}31$\times$. On NVIDIA H100 GPUs,
[TileMaxSim]{#abstract1.1.6 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} achieves
[80.2%]{#abstract1.1.7 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} of peak HBM bandwidth
(2,687 GB/s of 3,350 GB/s), approaching the utilization of memory-bound
GPU kernels like FlashAttention, and scores 82M documents/second on
synthetic uniform-length data (71.6M/s on real MS MARCO passages with
70% bandwidth utilization), yielding a [220$\times$]{#abstract1.1.8
.ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} speedup over loop-based scoring,
[6.5$\times$]{#abstract1.1.9 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} over fused
PyTorch baselines, [6.6--8.5$\times$]{#abstract1.1.10 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_bold} over
[torch.compile(mode=\"max-autotune\")]{#abstract1.1.11 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_typewriter}, and [469$\times$]{#abstract1.1.12 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_bold} higher scoring throughput than WARP's optimized CPU
engine, benchmarked on the same node. Crucially,
[TileMaxSim]{#abstract1.1.13 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} preserves
*exact* retrieval quality: on MS MARCO and three BEIR benchmarks,
rankings are identical to reference MaxSim. As a drop-in replacement in
ColBERTv2/PLAID's retrieval pipeline, [TileMaxSim]{#abstract1.1.15
.ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} reduces scoring latency at 100K
candidates from 268 ms to 1.2 ms, cutting end-to-end pipeline latency by
98%. The closest GPU competitor, a [torch.compile]{#abstract1.1.16
.ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}d batched-GEMM, is 6.6--8.5$\times$
slower because the compiler cannot fuse the matmul$\to$max$\to$sum
reduction. We further demonstrate constant throughput from 100K to 500K
documents, embarrassingly-parallel data-parallel multi-GPU sharding,
robustness across embedding dimensions from 64 to 768, and
precision-agnostic performance across FP16/BF16/FP32. Our IO complexity
analysis proves these kernels are asymptotically optimal for the GPU
memory hierarchy. Concurrent
work ([pony2026flashmaxsim](#bib.bib29){.ltx_ref}) independently
develops an IO-aware fused MaxSim kernel; we differ in our
dimension-tiling support for $d>128$ and fused product-quantization
scoring.
:::

[^1^[[^1^[footnotetext: ]{.ltx_note_type}Code available at
[https://github.com/ashutoshuiuc/tilemaxsim](https://github.com/ashutoshuiuc/tilemaxsim){.ltx_ref
.ltx_url
.ltx_font_typewriter}.]{.ltx_note_content}]{.ltx_note_outer}]{#footnotex1
.ltx_note .ltx_role_footnotetext}

::::::::::::: {#S1 .section .ltx_section}
## [1. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Introduction {#introduction .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S1.p1 .ltx_para}
Neural information retrieval has converged on multi-vector models as the
highest-quality paradigm for first-stage retrieval.
ColBERT ([khattab2020colbert](#bib.bib1){.ltx_ref}) and its
successors ([santhanam2022colbertv2](#bib.bib2){.ltx_ref}) represent
queries and documents as *sets* of token-level embeddings, computing
relevance via the MaxSim operator: the sum, over query tokens, of the
maximum cosine similarity with any document token. This fine-grained
interaction captures nuanced token-level matching signals that
single-vector models collapse into a single dot product, yielding
consistent quality gains over single-vector and BM25 baselines across
standard benchmarks ([khattab2020colbert](#bib.bib1){.ltx_ref};
[santhanam2022colbertv2](#bib.bib2){.ltx_ref}).
:::

::: {#S1.p2 .ltx_para}
The MaxSim scoring function, however, is computationally expensive: for
a query with $N_{q}$ tokens and a document with $N_{d}$ tokens, each of
dimension $d$, naive computation requires
$\mathcal{O}(N_{q}\cdot N_{d}\cdot d)$ multiply-accumulate operations.
At scale (scoring millions of candidate documents per query), this cost
dominates retrieval latency. Existing multi-vector engines have
addressed this challenge through *CPU-side* optimizations:
:::

::: {#S1.p3 .ltx_para}
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I1.i1}

  ::: {#S1.I1.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
  [PLAID]{#S1.I1.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
  .ltx_font_bold} ([santhanam2022plaid](#bib.bib3){.ltx_ref}) introduces
  centroid pruning and product quantization to reduce the number of
  full-precision similarity computations, achieving up to 7$\times$
  speedup over ColBERTv2.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I1.i2}

  ::: {#S1.I1.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
  [WARP]{#S1.I1.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
  .ltx_font_bold} ([scheerer2025warp](#bib.bib4){.ltx_ref}) designs
  optimized C++ scoring with implicit decompression and two-stage
  aggregation, achieving 3$\times$ speedup over PLAID.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I1.i3}

  ::: {#S1.I1.i3.p1 .ltx_para}
  [GEM]{#S1.I1.i3.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
  .ltx_font_bold} ([tian2026gem](#bib.bib5){.ltx_ref}) builds a
  graph-based index using Earth Mover's Distance for construction and
  Chamfer distance for search, with CPU-based OpenMP parallelism.
  :::
:::

::: {#S1.p4 .ltx_para}
All three systems confine MaxSim scoring to the CPU, a significant
fraction of per-query latency, particularly as candidate generation
improves ([scheerer2025warp](#bib.bib4){.ltx_ref}). We argue this is a
fundamental architectural mismatch: MaxSim is a batched matrix operation
perfectly suited for GPU parallelism, and modern GPUs offer
30--50$\times$ higher memory bandwidth than CPUs (3.35 TB/s on H100 vs.
$\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}100 GB/s for dual-channel DDR5). Crucially, our
contribution is *orthogonal* to these systems' indexing and candidate
generation innovations: [TileMaxSim]{#S1.p4.1.2 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_smallcaps} can serve as a drop-in replacement for the scoring
stage of any multi-vector retrieval pipeline.
:::

:::: {#S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### The Memory-Boundedness Insight. {#the-memory-boundedness-insight. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1 .ltx_para}
We perform a roofline analysis of MaxSim on modern GPUs and make a key
observation: [MaxSim is severely memory-bound]{#S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1
.ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. For typical ColBERT parameters ($N_{q}{=}32$,
$N_{d}{=}128$, $d{=}128$, $B{=}10^{4}$), the arithmetic intensity is
only 32.0 FLOP/byte for the fused operation and 16.1 FLOP/byte for the
naive implementation, far below the H100's compute-to-bandwidth
crossover of 591 FLOP/byte. This means GPU compute units sit idle
waiting for data from HBM, and the primary optimization target is
*reducing memory traffic*, not increasing compute throughput.
:::
::::

:::::: {#S1.SS0.SSS0.Px2 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Closing the Roofline Gap. {#closing-the-roofline-gap. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1 .ltx_para}
We design [TileMaxSim]{#S1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_smallcaps}, a family of IO-aware Triton kernels that close the
bandwidth gap by tiling the MaxSim computation to exploit the GPU memory
hierarchy. Compared to prior IO-aware kernels for
attention ([dao2022flashattention](#bib.bib7){.ltx_ref}) or learned
sparse retrieval ([sparton2026](#bib.bib22){.ltx_ref}), MaxSim has a
distinct reduction structure: (1) a max-reduction across document tokens
*per query token*, followed by an outer sum over query tokens. This
matmul$\to$max$\to$sum pattern differs from attention's
matmul$\to$softmax$\to$matmul (MaxSim needs only a running max, without
softmax's normalization and value re-weighting) and from sparse
retrieval's scatter-sum; (2) the same kernel must serve embedding
dimensions from $d{=}64$ to $d{=}768$, which we handle with a
dimension-tiled variant when $d$ exceeds the register-resident width of
our primary kernel (attention kernels instead keep $d$ resident and tile
the sequence dimension); and (3) the opportunity to fuse
product-quantization decompression with scoring, eliminating an entire
materialization step.
:::

::: {#S1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p2 .ltx_para}
Our contributions are:
:::

::: {#S1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p3 .ltx_para}
1.  [[(1)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I2.i1}

    ::: {#S1.I2.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Root-cause analysis of the bandwidth gap]{#S1.I2.i1.p1.1.1
    .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: A roofline analysis of MaxSim on modern
    GPUs showing that naive implementations achieve only 17.6% of peak
    bandwidth due to similarity matrix materialization, and quantifying
    the 2.0$\times$ IO reduction needed to reach the memory-bound
    ceiling ([[Section]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [2.3]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag}](#S2.SS3 "2.3. Roofline Analysis of MaxSim ‣ 2. Background and Motivation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}).
    :::
2.  [[(2)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I2.i2}

    ::: {#S1.I2.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Dimension tiling for $d>128$]{#S1.I2.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
    .ltx_font_bold}: A tiling strategy that partitions the embedding
    dimension into 128-wide chunks, so a single kernel family covers
    embeddings from $d{=}64$ to $d{=}768$ without exhausting
    register/SRAM capacity, achieving 86% of peak bandwidth at $d{=}768$
    ([[Section]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [3]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag}](#S3 "3. TileMaxSim Kernel Design ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}).
    :::
3.  [[(3)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I2.i3}

    ::: {#S1.I2.i3.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Fused PQ decompression+scoring]{#S1.I2.i3.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
    .ltx_font_bold}: [TileMaxSim-PQ]{#S1.I2.i3.p1.1.2 .ltx_text
    .ltx_font_smallcaps} scores PQ-compressed documents via
    shared-memory lookup tables without explicit decompression,
    achieving up to $\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}31$\times$ IO reduction over
    decompress-then-score pipelines ([[Section]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag} [4]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag}](#S4 "4. TileMaxSim-PQ: Fused PQ Scoring ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}).
    :::
4.  [[(4)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I2.i4}

    ::: {#S1.I2.i4.p1 .ltx_para}
    [80% peak bandwidth utilization]{#S1.I2.i4.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
    .ltx_font_bold}: Our multi-query tiled kernel
    ([TileMaxSim]{#S1.I2.i4.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} V2-MQ)
    achieves 80.2% of the H100's peak HBM bandwidth by reading each
    document embedding from HBM exactly once ([[Section]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag} [6]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag}](#S6 "6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}).
    For reference, FlashAttention-3 achieves $\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}75%
    of peak *compute* utilization; as a memory-bound kernel,
    [TileMaxSim]{#S1.I2.i4.p1.1.4 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} targets
    bandwidth rather than compute.
    :::
5.  [[(5)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I2.i5}

    ::: {#S1.I2.i5.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Exact quality preservation and comprehensive
    evaluation]{#S1.I2.i5.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: Identical
    rankings on MS MARCO and three BEIR benchmarks, 220$\times$ speedup
    over loop-based scoring, 469$\times$ scoring throughput over WARP
    (same-node benchmark), near-linear multi-GPU scaling, constant 83M
    docs/s throughput from 100K to 500K documents, and robustness across
    dimensions (64--768), precisions, token counts, and domains
    ([[Sections]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [6.10]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.SS10 "6.10. End-to-End Retrieval Quality ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
    and [[6]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag}](#S6 "6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}).
    :::
6.  [[(6)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I2.i6}

    ::: {#S1.I2.i6.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Drop-in system integration]{#S1.I2.i6.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
    .ltx_font_bold}: We demonstrate [TileMaxSim]{#S1.I2.i6.p1.1.2
    .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} as a direct replacement for
    ColBERTv2/PLAID's scoring kernel, reducing end-to-end pipeline
    latency by up to 98% at 100K candidates with identical retrieval
    quality ([[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [15]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T15 "In Drop-in Integration with PLAID/ColBERTv2. ‣ 6.11. Comparison with CPU Engines ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}).
    :::
:::
::::::
:::::::::::::

:::::::::::::::::::::: {#S2 .section .ltx_section}
## [2. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Background and Motivation {#background-and-motivation .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::::::: {#S2.SS1 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [2.1. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Multi-Vector Retrieval and MaxSim {#multi-vector-retrieval-and-maxsim .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S2.SS1.p1 .ltx_para}
ColBERT ([khattab2020colbert](#bib.bib1){.ltx_ref}) represents a query
$q$ as a set of $N_{q}$ token embeddings
$\mathbf{Q}=\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{N_{q}}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{q}\times d}$ and
a document $p$ as $N_{d}$ token embeddings
$\mathbf{D}=\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{N_{d}}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{d}\times d}$.
The relevance score is computed via MaxSim:
:::

::: {#S2.SS1.p2 .ltx_para}
  --------------------------------------------------- -- -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --
  [(1)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_equation .ltx_align_left}      $$\text{MaxSim}(\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{D})=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{q}}\max_{j=1}^{N_{d}}\;q_{i}^{\top}d_{j}$$   
  --------------------------------------------------- -- -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --
:::

::: {#S2.SS1.p3 .ltx_para}
This can be decomposed into two operations:

1.  [[(1)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S2.I1.i1}

    ::: {#S2.I1.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Similarity matrix]{#S2.I1.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}:
    $\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{D}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{q}\times N_{d}}$
    :::
2.  [[(2)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S2.I1.i2}

    ::: {#S2.I1.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Max-reduce + sum]{#S2.I1.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}:
    $\text{score}=\sum_{i}\max_{j}S_{ij}$
    :::
:::

::: {#S2.SS1.p4 .ltx_para}
In existing engines, step (1) is the bottleneck at scale because it must
be evaluated for every candidate document.
:::
:::::::

:::::: {#S2.SS2 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [2.2. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}GPU Memory Hierarchy {#gpu-memory-hierarchy .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S2.SS2.p1 .ltx_para}
Modern GPUs have a four-level memory hierarchy relevant to kernel
design:
:::

::: {#S2.SS2.p2 .ltx_para}
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S2.I2.i1}

  ::: {#S2.I2.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
  [Registers]{#S2.I2.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: Fastest,
  per-thread, $\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}256 KB per SM on H100. Used for
  accumulating running maxima.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S2.I2.i2}

  ::: {#S2.I2.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
  [Shared Memory (SRAM)]{#S2.I2.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}:
  228 KB per SM on H100, $\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}29.4 MB total across 132
  SMs. Bandwidth: $\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}19 TB/s aggregate.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S2.I2.i3}

  ::: {#S2.I2.i3.p1 .ltx_para}
  [L2 Cache]{#S2.I2.i3.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: 50 MB on H100.
  Transparently caches HBM accesses; particularly beneficial for
  [TileMaxSim-PQ]{#S2.I2.i3.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}'s
  distance tables which exhibit high temporal locality across documents.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S2.I2.i4}

  ::: {#S2.I2.i4.p1 .ltx_para}
  [HBM3]{#S2.I2.i4.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: 80 GB capacity,
  3.35 TB/s bandwidth. The primary bottleneck for memory-bound
  operations.
  :::
:::

::: {#S2.SS2.p3 .ltx_para}
The *compute-to-bandwidth ratio* of the H100 is:

  --------------------------------------------------- -- ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --
  [(2)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_equation .ltx_align_left}      $$\frac{\text{Peak FP16 TFLOP/s}}{\text{HBM Bandwidth}}=\frac{1979}{3.35}\approx 591\;\text{FLOP/byte}$$   
  --------------------------------------------------- -- ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --

Operations with arithmetic intensity (AI) below 591 FLOP/byte are
memory-bound.
:::
::::::

:::::::::::: {#S2.SS3 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [2.3. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Roofline Analysis of MaxSim {#roofline-analysis-of-maxsim .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S2.SS3.p1 .ltx_para}
We derive the arithmetic intensity for MaxSim with parameters $N_{q}$,
$N_{d}$, $d$, processing $B$ documents.
:::

:::: {#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px1 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Naive Implementation (materialize similarity matrix). {#naive-implementation-materialize-similarity-matrix. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1 .ltx_para}
  --------------------------------------------------- -- ------------------------------------------------- ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ --
  [(3)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_equation .ltx_align_left}      [FLOPs]{#S2.E3.2.1 .ltx_text .ltx_markedasmath}   $\displaystyle=B\cdot N_{q}\cdot N_{d}\cdot(\underbrace{2d}_{\text{dot product (mul+add)}}+\underbrace{1}_{\text{max cmp}})$                                                                                                   
  [(4)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_equation .ltx_align_left}      $\displaystyle\text{IO}_{\text{naive}}$           $\displaystyle=\underbrace{N_{q}\cdot d\cdot 2}_{\text{read }\mathbf{Q}}+\underbrace{B\cdot N_{d}\cdot d\cdot 2}_{\text{read }\mathbf{D}}+\underbrace{2\cdot B\cdot N_{q}\cdot N_{d}\cdot 4}_{\text{write+read }\mathbf{S}}$   
  --------------------------------------------------- -- ------------------------------------------------- ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ --

Embeddings are FP16 (the "$\cdot 2$" = 2 bytes/element); the similarity
matrix $\mathbf{S}$ is FP32 (4 bytes/element). The "$2\cdot$" on the
$\mathbf{S}$ term reflects that a non-fused einsum$\to$max pipeline
*writes* $\mathbf{S}$ to HBM after the matmul and *reads* it back for
the max-reduction. A fully-fused kernel
([TileMaxSim]{#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps})
eliminates both, keeping $\mathbf{S}$ in registers.
:::
::::

:::: {#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px2 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Fused Implementation ([TileMaxSim]{#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px2.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}). {#fused-implementation-tilemaxsim. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px2.p1 .ltx_para}
By tiling through SRAM, we avoid materializing $\mathbf{S}$:

  --------------------------------------------------- -- ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --
  [(5)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_equation .ltx_align_left}      $$\text{IO}_{\text{flash}}=N_{q}\cdot d\cdot 2+B\cdot N_{d}\cdot d\cdot 2+B\cdot N_{q}\cdot 4$$   
  --------------------------------------------------- -- ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --
:::
::::

:::::: {#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Arithmetic Intensity Comparison. {#arithmetic-intensity-comparison. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p1 .ltx_para}
For $N_{q}{=}32$, $N_{d}{=}128$, $d{=}128$, $B{=}10000$:
:::

::: {#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p2 .ltx_para}
+------------------------------------------+--------------------------------------+------------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+
| [Method]{#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p2.1.1.1.1     | [IO                                  | [AI                                      | [Bound]{#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p2.1.1.4.1 |
| .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}                | (bytes)]{#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p2.1.1.2.1 | (FLOP/byte)]{#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p2.1.1.3.1 | .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}           |
|                                          | .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}            | .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}                |                                     |
+------------------------------------------+--------------------------------------+------------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+
| Naive                                    | 655,368,192                          | 16.1                                     | Memory                              |
+------------------------------------------+--------------------------------------+------------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+
| [TileMaxSim]{#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p2.1.3.1.1 | 328,968,192                          | 32.0                                     | Memory                              |
| .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}           |                                      |                                          |                                     |
+------------------------------------------+--------------------------------------+------------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+
| *H100 crossover: 591 FLOP/byte*                                                                                                                                  |
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
:::

::: {#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p3 .ltx_para}
Both are deeply memory-bound, but [TileMaxSim]{#S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p3.1.1
.ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} reduces IO by 2.0$\times$, directly
translating to a 2.0$\times$ theoretical speedup. For larger $N_{q}$
(e.g., 64 tokens), the IO reduction increases to 3.0$\times$.
:::
::::::
::::::::::::
::::::::::::::::::::::

:::::::::::::::::::::::: {#S3 .section .ltx_section}
## [3. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}[TileMaxSim]{#S3.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} Kernel Design {#tilemaxsim-kernel-design .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::::: {#S3.SS1 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [3.1. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Design Principles {#design-principles .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S3.SS1.p1 .ltx_para}
Following the IO-aware paradigm of
FlashAttention ([dao2022flashattention](#bib.bib7){.ltx_ref}), we design
our kernels around three principles:
:::

::: {#S3.SS1.p2 .ltx_para}
1.  [[(1)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S3.I1.i1}

    ::: {#S3.I1.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Minimize HBM reads]{#S3.I1.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}:
    Load each embedding from HBM exactly once.
    :::
2.  [[(2)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S3.I1.i2}

    ::: {#S3.I1.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Maximize data reuse in SRAM]{#S3.I1.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
    .ltx_font_bold}: Keep frequently-accessed data (query embeddings,
    running maxima) in registers and shared memory.
    :::
3.  [[(3)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S3.I1.i3}

    ::: {#S3.I1.i3.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Fuse operations]{#S3.I1.i3.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}:
    Combine matmul, max-reduction, and sum-reduction into a single
    kernel pass to avoid intermediate HBM writes.
    :::
:::
:::::

::::::::::::: {#S3.SS2 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [3.2. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Kernel Variants {#kernel-variants .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S3.SS2.p1 .ltx_para}
We implement three tiling strategies, each optimal for different
workload characteristics.
:::

::::: {#S3.SS2.SSS1 .section .ltx_subsubsection}
#### [3.2.1. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsubsection}V1: Per-Query-Token Tiling {#v1-per-query-token-tiling .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsubsection}

::: {#S3.SS2.SSS1.p1 .ltx_para}
The simplest strategy assigns one thread block per (batch, query-token)
pair. Each block:

1.  [[(1)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S3.I2.i1}

    ::: {#S3.I2.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
    Loads one query token $q_{i}$ into registers ($d$ values).
    :::
2.  [[(2)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S3.I2.i2}

    ::: {#S3.I2.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
    Iterates over document token tiles of size
    [BLOCK_Nd]{#S3.I2.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}.
    :::
3.  [[(3)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S3.I2.i3}

    ::: {#S3.I2.i3.p1 .ltx_para}
    For each tile, loads $\texttt{BLOCK\_Nd}\times d$ document
    embeddings into shared memory.
    :::
4.  [[(4)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S3.I2.i4}

    ::: {#S3.I2.i4.p1 .ltx_para}
    Computes dot products and updates a running maximum in a register.
    :::
:::

::: {#S3.SS2.SSS1.p2 .ltx_para}
[IO analysis]{#S3.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: Each
document tile is loaded once per query token, so total reads are
$N_{q}\times(N_{d}\cdot d\cdot 2)$ for documents plus
$N_{q}\cdot d\cdot 2$ for queries. This is $N_{q}\times$ more document
reads than optimal.
:::
:::::

::::: {#S3.SS2.SSS2 .section .ltx_subsubsection}

::: {#S3.SS2.SSS2.p1 .ltx_para}
To eliminate redundant document reads, V2 assigns one program per
document:

class="math inline"><em>N</em><sub><em>q</em></sub> − 1</span> <span
id="alg2.l4.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">do</span>
</div>
<div id="alg2.l5" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg2.l5.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">5:</span></span>   Load <span
class="math inline"><em>q</em><sub><em>i</em></sub> ← <strong>Q</strong>[<em>i</em>,  : ]</span>
to registers
</div>
<div id="alg2.l6" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg2.l6.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">6:</span></span>   <span
class="math inline"><em>m</em><sub><em>i</em></sub> ← −∞</span>
</div>
<div id="alg2.l7" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg2.l7.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">7:</span></span>   <span
id="alg2.l7.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">for</span> <span
class="math inline"><em>t</em> = 0</span> <span id="alg2.l7.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">to</span> <span
class="math inline">⌈<em>N</em><sub><em>d</em></sub>/<code>BLOCK_Nd</code>⌉ − 1</span>
<span id="alg2.l7.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">do</span>
</div>
<div id="alg2.l8" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg2.l8.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">8:</span></span>    Load
document tile <span
class="math inline"><strong>D</strong><sub><em>t</em></sub></span> to
SRAM
</div>
<div id="alg2.l9" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg2.l9.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">9:</span></span>    <span
class="math inline"><em>m</em><sub><em>i</em></sub> ← max (<em>m</em><sub><em>i</em></sub>, max (<strong>D</strong><sub><em>t</em></sub> ⋅ <em>q</em><sub><em>i</em></sub>))</span>
</div>
<div id="alg2.l10" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg2.l10.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">10:</span></span>   <span
id="alg2.l10.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">end</span> <span
id="alg2.l10.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">for</span>
</div>
<div id="alg2.l11" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg2.l11.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">11:</span></span>   <span
class="math inline"><em>s</em> ← <em>s</em> + <em>m</em><sub><em>i</em></sub></span>
</div>
<div id="alg2.l12" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg2.l12.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">12:</span></span>  <span
id="alg2.l12.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">end</span> <span
id="alg2.l12.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">for</span>
</div>
<div id="alg2.l13" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg2.l13.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">13:</span></span>  Store <span
class="math inline"><em>s</em></span> to <span
class="math inline">scores[<em>b</em>]</span>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_float"><span id="alg2.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Algorithm 2</span> </span> <span
id="alg2.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">TileMaxSim</span> V2:
Per-Document Kernel</figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S3.SS2.SSS2.p2 .ltx_para}
This reads each document embedding $N_{q}$ times (once per query token)
but avoids the intermediate token-max buffer. The sum-reduction is fused
into the same kernel.
:::
:::::

::::: {#S3.SS2.SSS3 .section .ltx_subsubsection}
#### [3.2.3. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsubsection}V2-MQ: Multi-Query Tiling (Optimal) {#v2-mq-multi-query-tiling-optimal .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsubsection}

::: {#S3.SS2.SSS3.p1 .ltx_para}
Our best-performing variant tiles over *both* query and document tokens
simultaneously:
:::

<figure id="alg3"
class="ltx_float ltx_float_algorithm ltx_framed ltx_framed_top">
<div id="alg3.6" class="ltx_listing">
<div id="alg3.l1" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg3.l1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">1:</span></span>  <span
id="alg3.l1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Grid</span>: <span
class="math inline">(<em>B</em>, ⌈<em>N</em><sub><em>q</em></sub>/<code>BQ</code>⌉)</span>
programs
</div>
<div id="alg3.l2" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg3.l2.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">2:</span></span>  <span
class="math inline"><em>b</em> ← program_id(0)</span>, <span
class="math inline"><em>q</em><em>b</em> ← program_id(1)</span>
</div>
<div id="alg3.l3" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg3.l3.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">3:</span></span>  Load <span
class="math inline"><strong>Q</strong><sub>tile</sub> ← <strong>Q</strong>[<em>q</em><em>b</em> ⋅ <code>BQ</code> : (<em>q</em><em>b</em> + 1) ⋅ <code>BQ</code>,  : ]</span>
{<span class="math inline"><code>BQ</code> × <em>d</em></span> in
registers}
</div>
<div id="alg3.l4" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg3.l4.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">4:</span></span>  <span
class="math inline"><strong>m</strong> ← [−∞, …, −∞]</span> {<span
id="alg3.l4.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_markedasmath ltx_font_typewriter">BQ</span> running
maxes}
</div>
<div id="alg3.l5" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg3.l5.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">5:</span></span>  <span
id="alg3.l5.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">for</span> <span
class="math inline"><em>t</em> = 0</span> <span id="alg3.l5.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">to</span> <span
class="math inline">⌈<em>N</em><sub><em>d</em></sub>/<code>BN</code>⌉ − 1</span>
<span id="alg3.l5.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">do</span>
</div>
<div id="alg3.l6" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg3.l6.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">6:</span></span>   Load <span
class="math inline"><strong>D</strong><sub><em>t</em></sub></span> to
SRAM {<span class="math inline"><code>BN</code> × <em>d</em></span>,
loaded <span id="alg3.l6.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">once</span>
for <span id="alg3.l6.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_markedasmath ltx_font_typewriter">BQ</span> query
tokens}
</div>
<div id="alg3.l7" class="ltx_listingline">

class="math inline"><strong>S</strong><sub><em>t</em></sub> ← <strong>Q</strong><sub>tile</sub> ⋅ <strong>D</strong><sub><em>t</em></sub><sup>⊤</sup></span>
{<span class="math inline"><code>BQ</code> × <code>BN</code></span> via

<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg3.l9.1"

<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg3.l10.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">10:</span></span>  <span
id="alg3.l10.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">atomic_add</span>(<span
class="math inline">scores[<em>b</em>]</span>, <span
class="math inline">∑<strong>m</strong></span>)
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_float"><span id="alg3.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Algorithm 3</span> </span> <span
id="alg3.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">TileMaxSim</span> V2-MQ:
Multi-Query Tiling</figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S3.SS2.SSS3.p2 .ltx_para}
[Key optimization]{#S3.SS2.SSS3.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}
([[Figure]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [1]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.F1 "In 3.3. Data-Flow Summary ‣ 3. TileMaxSim Kernel Design ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}):
Each document tile is loaded from HBM only
$\lceil N_{q}/\texttt{BQ}\rceil$ times instead of $N_{q}$ times (the
per-query-token baseline). For $\texttt{BQ}{=}16$ and $N_{q}{=}32$, that
is 2 reads instead of 32 (a 16$\times$ reduction); setting
$\texttt{BQ}{=}N_{q}{=}32$ reaches the optimal single read. The
[tl.dot]{#S3.SS2.SSS3.p2.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter} operation
maps directly to Tensor Core matrix-multiply, achieving near-peak
compute throughput on the $\texttt{BQ}\times\texttt{BN}$ tile.
:::
:::::
:::::::::::::

:::: {#S3.SS3 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [3.3. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Data-Flow Summary {#data-flow-summary .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S3.SS3.p1 .ltx_para}
[[Figure]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [1]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.F1 "In 3.3. Data-Flow Summary ‣ 3. TileMaxSim Kernel Design ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
summarizes the V2-MQ memory hierarchy traversal: query tiles are loaded
into SRAM once, document tiles stream through one block at a time, and
all partial similarities, maxima, and the final sum are kept in
registers, so no intermediate tensor is written back to HBM.
:::

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S3.F1.5"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 1</span>. </span><span
id="S3.F1.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Data flow of <span
id="S3.F1.6.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">TileMaxSim</span>
V2-MQ. Query tiles are loaded once into SRAM; document tiles stream
through SRAM one block at a time. Partial similarities are computed via
Tensor Core <span id="S3.F1.6.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">tl.dot</span> and reduced to
running maxima in registers. No intermediate data is written back to
HBM.</span></figcaption>
</figure>
::::

::::::: {#S3.SS4 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [3.4. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}IO Complexity {#io-complexity .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

:::: {#S3.Thmtheorem1 .ltx_theorem .ltx_theorem_theorem}
###### Theorem 1 (TileMaxSim IO Complexity). {#theorem-1-tilemaxsim-io-complexity. .ltx_title .ltx_runin .ltx_font_smallcaps .ltx_title_theorem}

::: {#S3.Thmtheorem1.p1 .ltx_para}
[For scoring $B$ documents with $N_{q}$ query tokens and $N_{d}$
document tokens of dimension $d$, the HBM IO of
[TileMaxSim]{#S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}
V2-MQ with tile sizes [BQ]{#S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.1.1.2 .ltx_text
.ltx_markedasmath .ltx_font_typewriter} and
[BN]{#S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.1.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_markedasmath
.ltx_font_typewriter} is:]{#S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_italic}

  --------------------------------------------------- -- ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --
  [(6)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_equation .ltx_align_left}      $$\text{IO}=\Big(\underbrace{N_{q}\cdot d}_{\text{read }\mathbf{Q}\text{ once}}+\underbrace{\lceil N_{q}/\texttt{BQ}\rceil\cdot B\cdot N_{d}\cdot d}_{\text{read }\mathbf{D}\text{ per query block}}\Big)\cdot 2+B\cdot 4$$   
  --------------------------------------------------- -- ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --

[When $\texttt{BQ}=N_{q}$ (all query tokens processed together), this
reduces to:]{#S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.2.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}

  --------------------------------------------------- -- ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --
  [(7)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_equation .ltx_align_left}      $$\text{IO}_{\text{optimal}}=(N_{q}\cdot d+B\cdot N_{d}\cdot d)\cdot 2+B\cdot 4$$   
  --------------------------------------------------- -- ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --

[which is optimal: every embedding is read exactly once from
HBM.]{#S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.3.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}
:::
::::

:::: {#S3.SS4.2 .ltx_proof}
###### Proof. {#proof. .ltx_title .ltx_runin .ltx_font_italic .ltx_title_proof}

::: {#S3.SS4.p1 .ltx_para}
[Each of the $\lceil N_{q}/\texttt{BQ}\rceil$ query-token blocks reads
all $B\cdot\lceil N_{d}/\texttt{BN}\rceil$ document tiles, each of size
$\texttt{BN}\times d\times 2$ bytes, so total document reads are
$\lceil N_{q}/\texttt{BQ}\rceil\cdot B\cdot N_{d}\cdot d\cdot 2$. Each
query block reads only its own [BQ]{#S3.SS4.p1.1.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_markedasmath .ltx_font_typewriter} query tokens
($\texttt{BQ}\cdot d\cdot 2$ bytes), so summed over all
$\lceil N_{q}/\texttt{BQ}\rceil$ blocks the query reads total
$\lceil N_{q}/\texttt{BQ}\rceil\cdot\texttt{BQ}\cdot d\cdot 2=N_{q}\cdot d\cdot 2$
(each query token read once). Output is $B\times 4$ bytes (one float per
document). Setting $\texttt{BQ}=N_{q}$ yields one pass over documents.
∎]{#S3.SS4.p1.1.1 .ltx_text}
:::
::::
:::::::
::::::::::::::::::::::::

:::::::::::::::::::: {#S4 .section .ltx_section}
## [4. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}[TileMaxSim-PQ]{#S4.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}: Fused PQ Scoring {#tilemaxsim-pq-fused-pq-scoring .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

:::::: {#S4.SS1 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [4.1. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Product Quantization for ColBERT {#product-quantization-for-colbert .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S4.SS1.p1 .ltx_para}
Production ColBERT
systems ([santhanam2022colbertv2](#bib.bib2){.ltx_ref};
[scheerer2025warp](#bib.bib4){.ltx_ref}) compress token embeddings using
product quantization (PQ). The $d$-dimensional vector is split into $M$
sub-vectors of dimension $d_{\text{sub}}=d/M$, each quantized to one of
$K$ centroids. A document token is stored as $M$ code bytes, reducing
storage from $d\times 2$ bytes (FP16) to $M$ bytes.
:::

::: {#S4.SS1.p2 .ltx_para}
The standard scoring pipeline is:

1.  [[(1)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S4.I1.i1}

    ::: {#S4.I1.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Decompress]{#S4.I1.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: For each
    document token, look up $M$ centroids and concatenate to reconstruct
    the approximate vector.
    :::
2.  [[(2)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S4.I1.i2}

    ::: {#S4.I1.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Score]{#S4.I1.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: Compute MaxSim
    on the decompressed vectors.
    :::
:::

::: {#S4.SS1.p3 .ltx_para}
This decompress-then-score approach writes $B\cdot N_{d}\cdot d\cdot 2$
bytes of decompressed vectors to HBM, a significant overhead.
:::
::::::

::::: {#S4.SS2 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [4.2. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Lookup-Table Scoring {#lookup-table-scoring .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S4.SS2.p1 .ltx_para}
The lookup-table principle itself is the classical asymmetric distance
computation (ADC) of product quantization: scoring PQ codes against a
per-query distance table without reconstructing vectors, as also used by
WARP's implicit decompression ([scheerer2025warp](#bib.bib4){.ltx_ref}).
Our contribution is not the lookup idea but fusing it into an IO-aware
GPU MaxSim kernel that keeps the table in SRAM/L2 and never writes
decompressed vectors to HBM. Concretely, [TileMaxSim-PQ]{#S4.SS2.p1.1.1
.ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} pre-computes a *distance table*:

  --------------------------------------------------- -- ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --
  [(8)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_equation .ltx_align_left}      $$T[i,m,k]=q_{i}[m\cdot d_{\text{sub}}:(m+1)\cdot d_{\text{sub}}]^{\top}\cdot C[m,k]$$   
  --------------------------------------------------- -- ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- --

where $C[m,k]\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\text{sub}}}$ is the $k$-th centroid of
the $m$-th sub-quantizer, and $q_{i}$ is the $i$-th query token. The
table has $N_{q}\times M\times K$ entries.
:::

::: {#S4.SS2.p2 .ltx_para}
The similarity between query token $i$ and a PQ-compressed document
token with codes $(c_{1},\ldots,c_{M})$ is:

  --------------------------------------------------- -- ---------------------------------------------------------------- --
  [(9)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_equation .ltx_align_left}      $$q_{i}^{\top}\hat{d}_{j}\approx\sum_{m=1}^{M}T[i,m,c_{j,m}]$$   
  --------------------------------------------------- -- ---------------------------------------------------------------- --
:::
:::::

:::::::: {#S4.SS3 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [4.3. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Kernel Design {#kernel-design .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S4.SS3.p1 .ltx_para}
[TileMaxSim-PQ]{#S4.SS3.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} operates
in two phases:
:::

:::: {#S4.SS3.SSS0.Px1 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Phase 1: Table Construction. {#phase-1-table-construction. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S4.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1 .ltx_para}
A grid of $(N_{q},M)$ programs, each computing $K$ dot products of
dimension $d_{\text{sub}}$. Total FLOPs:
$N_{q}\cdot M\cdot K\cdot 2d_{\text{sub}}$ (the "$2$" is one multiply
and one add per dimension). The per-query table is
$N_{q}\times M\times K\times 4$ bytes; for $N_{q}{=}32$, $M{=}16$,
$K{=}256$ this is 512 KB, which fits comfortably in the H100's 50 MB L2
cache during that query's scoring phase, so the dominant table accesses
in Phase 2 hit L2 rather than HBM.
:::
::::

:::: {#S4.SS3.SSS0.Px2 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Phase 2: Fused Lookup + Max + Sum. {#phase-2-fused-lookup-max-sum. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S4.SS3.SSS0.Px2.p1 .ltx_para}
Each program handles one (batch, query-token) pair. For each document
token tile, it loads $M$ PQ codes (1 byte each), performs $M$ table
lookups, accumulates the score, and updates a running maximum. The table
entries are accessed with high locality since all documents share the
same table.
:::
::::
::::::::

:::::: {#S4.SS4 .section .ltx_subsection}

::: {#S4.SS4.p2 .ltx_para}
For large batch sizes ($B\geq 10$K), [TileMaxSim-PQ]{#S4.SS4.p2.1.1
.ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} achieves up to

:::

::::::::::: {#S5 .section .ltx_section}
## [5. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Related Work {#related-work .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

:::: {#S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Multi-Vector Retrieval Engines. {#multi-vector-retrieval-engines. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1 .ltx_para}
PLAID ([santhanam2022plaid](#bib.bib3){.ltx_ref}) introduced centroid
pruning and residual compression for ColBERT, achieving up to 7$\times$
latency reduction. PLAID includes a CUDA kernel for centroid-based
approximate decompression; final scoring runs in PyTorch. Our work
differs in three ways: (1) we compute *exact* MaxSim (not
centroid-approximate), (2) our kernel is IO-aware with explicit SRAM
tiling achieving 80% peak HBM bandwidth (vs. PLAID's unreported
bandwidth utilization), and (3) we support arbitrary embedding
dimensions via dimension tiling.
WARP ([scheerer2025warp](#bib.bib4){.ltx_ref}) further optimized CPU
scoring with SIMD-accelerated C++ kernels, achieving a 41$\times$
end-to-end speedup over XTR's reference implementation via implicit
decompression through lookup tables that avoid materializing full
embeddings, and dynamic similarity imputation, yielding 3$\times$ over
PLAID. WARP's contribution is CPU-optimized: implicit decompression
through lookup tables and a two-stage reduction with cache-friendly
access patterns on commodity hardware. Our contribution is orthogonal: a
GPU-native IO-aware MaxSim kernel that reaches 80% of peak HBM bandwidth
on NVIDIA H100s. Where WARP avoids materializing embeddings via
lookup-table scoring, we avoid materializing the full
$N_{q}\times N_{d}$ similarity matrix via SRAM tiling; different
bottlenecks addressed by different strategies for different hardware
targets. GEM ([tian2026gem](#bib.bib5){.ltx_ref}) proposed a graph-based
index using metric decoupling (EMD for construction, Chamfer for
search), reporting several-fold speedups over PLAID.
IGP ([igp2025](#bib.bib6){.ltx_ref}) introduced proximity graph indexing
with similar goals.
ColBERT-serve ([colbertserve2025](#bib.bib16){.ltx_ref}) applies
memory-mapping to the ColBERT index to reduce RAM usage by 90%, enabling
deployment on commodity hardware.
ESPN ([espn2024](#bib.bib17){.ltx_ref}) addresses the SSD-to-memory
bottleneck for multi-vector retrieval via prefetching and hardware
acceleration. EMVB ([emvb2024](#bib.bib27){.ltx_ref}) accelerates
ColBERT retrieval via bitvector prefiltering and product quantization,
reducing memory footprint and enabling fast candidate generation, but
delegates final MaxSim scoring to standard (non-IO-aware) CPU or GPU
operations. All of these systems confine MaxSim scoring to the CPU or
use non-optimized GPU operations; our work targets the complementary
dimension of bandwidth-optimal, IO-aware GPU-accelerated scoring.
:::
::::

:::: {#S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Approximation Approaches. {#approximation-approaches. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1 .ltx_para}
XTR ([lee2024xtr](#bib.bib12){.ltx_ref}) approximates MaxSim via
token-level retrieval, MUVERA ([muvera2024](#bib.bib13){.ltx_ref})
projects multi-vector to fixed-dimensional representations, and binary
quantization approaches ([vespa2024bq](#bib.bib14){.ltx_ref}) trade
accuracy for speed. Recent work on pruning multi-vector representations
for visual document retrieval ([prune2026](#bib.bib18){.ltx_ref})
reduces token count to lower scoring cost. These are complementary to
our exact/PQ scoring kernels.
:::
::::

:::: {#S5.SS0.SSS0.Px3 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### IO-Aware GPU Algorithms. {#io-aware-gpu-algorithms. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S5.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1 .ltx_para}
FlashAttention ([dao2022flashattention](#bib.bib7){.ltx_ref};
[dao2023flashattention2](#bib.bib8){.ltx_ref}) demonstrated that
IO-aware kernel design yields up to 3$\times$ wall-clock speedups for
attention by tiling through SRAM.
Flash-Decoding ([flashdecoding2023](#bib.bib9){.ltx_ref}) extended this
to long-context decoding. IO-aware fused kernels were applied beyond
attention early on. FlashConv in Hungry Hungry Hippos
(H3) ([fu2023h3](#bib.bib28){.ltx_ref}) targets state-space and
long-convolution models, and
FlashFFTConv ([flashfftconv2024](#bib.bib26){.ltx_ref}) later fuses FFT
convolutions with Tensor Core--friendly matrix decompositions for
long-sequence modeling.
FlashSinkhorn ([flashsinkhorn2026](#bib.bib10){.ltx_ref}) applied
similar ideas to optimal transport, and
FlashSFA ([flashsfa2026](#bib.bib19){.ltx_ref}) extends the IO-aware
paradigm to sparse feature attention.
Sparton ([sparton2026](#bib.bib22){.ltx_ref}) applies fused Triton
kernels to learned sparse retrieval (SPLADE), tiling along batch and
vocabulary dimensions. Our work targets the multi-vector MaxSim
operation, whose matmul$\to$max$\to$sum reduction differs from SPLADE's
scatter-sum: it requires (1) a per-query-token max-reduction followed by
an outer sum, (2) a dimension-tiled variant to span $d{\in}[64,768]$ in
one kernel family, and (3) fused PQ decompression that Sparton does not
address. Industrial systems like Meta's
Andromeda ([andromeda2024](#bib.bib23){.ltx_ref}) use IO-aware operators
with deep kernel fusion for ads retrieval, but architectural details are
proprietary and the workload targets single-vector scoring rather than
multi-vector MaxSim. We bring the IO-aware paradigm to multi-vector
retrieval scoring, showing that MaxSim's matmul-max-sum reduction
pattern admits different tiling strategies than attention or sparse
retrieval.
:::
::::

:::: {#S5.SS0.SSS0.Px4 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### GPU-Accelerated Retrieval. {#gpu-accelerated-retrieval. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S5.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1 .ltx_para}
NVIDIA's cuVS library provides GPU-accelerated ANN search via
CAGRA ([cagra2023](#bib.bib11){.ltx_ref}), IVF-PQ, and HNSW indices, but
only for *single-vector* distance computation.
FAISS ([faiss](#bib.bib21){.ltx_ref}) offers GPU flat and IVF-PQ
indices, again for single vectors. Neither can natively compute MaxSim's
matmul-max-sum reduction over sets of embeddings; adapting them requires
materializing the full $N_{q}\times N_{d}$ similarity matrix, which is
exactly the bottleneck our IO-aware kernels eliminate.
VecFlow-Chamfer ([vecflow2026](#bib.bib24){.ltx_ref}) is a concurrent
GPU-native system for multi-vector search on NVIDIA superchips (Grace
Hopper). It casts multi-vector scoring as Chamfer similarity
(mathematically equivalent to MaxSim) and builds a full data management
layer with GPU-resident indices and NVLink-optimized data movement. Our
work differs in focus: where VecFlow-Chamfer targets the systems stack
(indexing, memory management, multi-GPU orchestration),
[TileMaxSim]{#S5.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}
targets the *kernel*-level IO complexity of the scoring operator itself,
providing roofline analysis, SRAM tiling strategies, dimension tiling
for $d>128$, and fused PQ decompression, concerns orthogonal to
VecFlow-Chamfer's system design. Bian et
al. ([bian2026gpu](#bib.bib25){.ltx_ref}) concurrently propose a GPU
co-processing engine for multi-vector retrieval, extending their
graph-based IGP index ([igp2025](#bib.bib6){.ltx_ref}) with GPU
acceleration. Most directly related, Pony et
al. ([pony2026flashmaxsim](#bib.bib29){.ltx_ref}) concurrently propose
an IO-aware fused GPU kernel for MaxSim that, like ours, avoids
materializing the $N_{q}\times N_{d}$ similarity tensor and serves as a
drop-in, quality-exact replacement; they additionally fuse the training
backward pass and use an INT8 compression path. Our work is
complementary and distinct in two respects they do not address:
*dimension tiling* for $d>128$ embeddings, and *fused
product-quantization* (ADC-style) scoring rather than INT8. We developed
this work independently and concurrently. Recent work on GPU-centric
storage access ([gpu_ir2025](#bib.bib15){.ltx_ref}) addresses a
complementary dimension (data movement from SSD to GPU).
AutoKernel ([autokernel2026](#bib.bib20){.ltx_ref}) explores LLM-driven
GPU kernel optimization, which could further improve our Triton kernels
via automated tuning.
:::
::::
:::::::::::

:::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::: {#S6 .section .ltx_section}
## [6. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Evaluation {#evaluation .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::::::::::: {#S6.SS1 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [6.1. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Experimental Setup {#experimental-setup .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

:::: {#S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Hardware. {#hardware. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1 .ltx_para}
Two NVIDIA H100 SXM 80GB GPUs with HBM3 (3.35 TB/s bandwidth each), 132
SMs, 228 KB shared memory per SM, FP16 Tensor Core peak of 1979 TFLOP/s.
All single-GPU experiments use one H100; multi-GPU experiments use both.
:::
::::

:::: {#S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Software. {#software. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1 .ltx_para}
PyTorch 2.6, Triton 3.2, CUDA 12.4. All timing uses
[torch.cuda.Event]{#S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_typewriter} for precise GPU-side measurement. Default
precision is FP16 embeddings with FP32 accumulation.
:::
::::

:::: {#S6.SS1.SSS0.Px3 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Workload Parameters. {#workload-parameters. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1 .ltx_para}
Following ColBERT conventions: $d{=}128$ embedding dimension,
$N_{q}{=}32$ query tokens, $N_{d}\in\{64,128,256\}$ document tokens.
Batch sizes (number of candidate documents to score) range from 100 to
100K.
:::
::::

:::: {#S6.SS1.SSS0.Px4 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Baselines. {#baselines. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS1.SSS0.Px4.p1 .ltx_para}

  ::: {#S6.I1.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
  [PyTorch Naive]{#S6.I1.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}:
  [torch.einsum]{#S6.I1.i1.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter} to
  compute full similarity matrix, then max-reduce and sum.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I1.i2}

  ::: {#S6.I1.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
  [PyTorch Loop]{#S6.I1.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: Iterate
  over query tokens, computing one row of similarities at a time (avoids
  materializing full matrix).
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I1.i3}

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table"><span id="S6.T1.4"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Table 1</span>. </span><span
id="S6.T1.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">MaxSim scoring
latency (ms) and throughput (docs/sec) on H100. <span
class="math inline"><em>N</em><sub><em>q</em></sub> = 32</span>, <span
class="math inline"><em>d</em> = 128</span>. V2-MQ uses <span
class="math inline"><code>BQ</code> = 32</span> (single-pass, optimal
IO). Best kernel result in <span id="S6.T1.5.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">bold</span>. All timings via CUDA events
(50 runs).</span></figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S6.SS2.p1 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [1]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T1 "In 6.2. MaxSim Scoring Throughput ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
presents the main results. Key findings:
:::

::: {#S6.SS2.p2 .ltx_para}
1.  [[(1)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I2.i1}

    ::: {#S6.I2.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
    [220$\times$ over PyTorch Loop]{#S6.I2.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
    .ltx_font_bold}: At $N_{d}{=}128$, $B{=}100$K,
    [TileMaxSim]{#S6.I2.i1.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} V2-MQ
    achieves 81.2M docs/s vs. 372K docs/s for the loop baseline (1.23 ms
    vs. 268.6 ms). The loop baseline suffers from Python loop overhead
    and poor GPU utilization due to 32 sequential kernel launches.
    :::
2.  [[(2)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I2.i2}

    ::: {#S6.I2.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
    [6.5$\times$ over PyTorch Naive]{#S6.I2.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
    .ltx_font_bold}: When PyTorch Naive fits in memory
    (B$\leq$`<!-- -->`{=html}10K), V2-MQ achieves a substantial speedup
    by avoiding similarity matrix materialization. For $N_{d}{=}128$,
    $B{=}10$K: 60.4M vs. 9.3M docs/s.
    :::
3.  [[(3)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I2.i3}

    ::: {#S6.I2.i3.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Constant throughput at scale]{#S6.I2.i3.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
    .ltx_font_bold}: V2-MQ maintains near-constant throughput (82--83M
    docs/s for $N_{d}{=}128$) from $B{=}100$K to $B{=}500$K
    ([[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [8]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T8 "In Batch Scaling (1K to 500K documents). ‣ 6.8. Scaling Analysis ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}),
    indicating full GPU utilization with 80%+ of peak HBM bandwidth.
    PyTorch Naive runs out of memory at $B{=}100$K.
    :::
4.  [[(4)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I2.i4}

    ::: {#S6.I2.i4.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Single-pass optimality]{#S6.I2.i4.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}:
    Setting $\texttt{BQ}{=}N_{q}{=}32$ processes all query tokens in one
    pass, reading each document embedding from HBM *exactly once*. This
    optimal IO pattern yields 4.5$\times$ higher throughput than the
    prior $\texttt{BQ}{=}16$ configuration.
    :::
5.  [[(5)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I2.i5}

    ::: {#S6.I2.i5.p1 .ltx_para}
    [Faster than the compiler]{#S6.I2.i5.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
    .ltx_font_bold}: As a strong automatic-fusion baseline we benchmark
    [torch.compile]{#S6.I2.i5.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}
    ([max-autotune]{#S6.I2.i5.p1.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}
    mode), which applies operator fusion, CUDA-graph capture, and Triton
    autotuning. Across configurations, [TileMaxSim]{#S6.I2.i5.p1.1.4
    .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} is consistently 6.6--8.5$\times$
    faster ([[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [2]{.ltx_text
    .ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T2 "In 6.2. MaxSim Scoring Throughput ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}).
    The compiler cannot discover the matmul $\to$ max $\to$ sum fusion
    because it operates on individual operators rather than the
    composite reduction.
    :::
:::

id="S6.T3.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Throughput (M
docs/s) comparison across kernel variants. <span
class="math inline"><em>N</em><sub><em>q</em></sub> = 32</span>, <span
class="math inline"><em>d</em> = 128</span>, <span
class="math inline"><em>B</em> = 100</span>K.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S6.SS3.p1 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T3 "In 6.3. Kernel Variant Comparison ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
shows that V2-MQ's multi-query tiling with $\texttt{BQ}{=}32$ provides a
[14.1$\times$]{#S6.SS3.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} throughput
improvement over V1 for $N_{d}{=}128$, $B{=}100$K. The advantage stems
from V2-MQ reading each document embedding from HBM exactly once (vs.
$N_{q}{=}32$ times for V1). V2 (per-document) performs poorly for large
$N_{d}$ because a single program must sequentially process all
$N_{q}\times N_{d}$ pairs without parallelism.
:::
::::

::::: {#S6.SS4 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [6.4. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Comparison Against GPU Baselines {#comparison-against-gpu-baselines .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S6.SS4.p1 .ltx_para}
The comparisons above are against our own PyTorch baselines. To position
[TileMaxSim]{#S6.SS4.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} against a
*real* GPU MaxSim implementation, we benchmark the exact scoring path
used by ColBERTv2/PLAID ([colbert_score]{#S6.SS4.p1.1.3 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_typewriter}: a batched [D @ Q.T]{#S6.SS4.p1.1.4 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_typewriter} followed by a max-over-tokens and sum-over-query
reduction), which materializes the full $N_{q}\times N_{d}$ similarity
matrix in HBM. We also benchmark [torch.compile]{#S6.SS4.p1.1.5
.ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter} ([max-autotune]{#S6.SS4.p1.1.6 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_typewriter}) as the strongest automatic-fusion competitor. All
three run on the same H100 with identical L2-normalized FP16 inputs,
timed with CUDA events (50 runs).
:::

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table"><span id="S6.T4.5"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Table 4</span>. </span><span
id="S6.T4.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Directly measured
GPU-vs-GPU comparison on one H100. <span
class="math inline"><em>N</em><sub><em>q</em></sub> = 32</span>, <span
class="math inline"><em>d</em> = 128</span>, L2-normalized FP16 inputs,
CUDA events (50 runs). “PLAID GPU” is ColBERT’s <span id="S6.T4.6.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">colbert_score</span> path
(materialize <span class="math inline"><strong>S</strong></span>, then
max+sum). Speedups are <span id="S6.T4.6.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">TileMaxSim</span> V2-MQ over each
baseline. Rankings match: top-100 overlap is 98–100/100 and max absolute
score error vs. PLAID is <span
class="math inline"> ≤ 4.6 × 10<sup>−3</sup></span> (PLAID’s fp16 matmul
is itself less accurate than our fp32 accumulation).</span></figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S6.SS4.p2 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [4]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T4 "In 6.4. Comparison Against GPU Baselines ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
shows that against PLAID's actual GPU scoring path,
[TileMaxSim]{#S6.SS4.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} is
[1.7--1.9$\times$]{#S6.SS4.p2.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} faster for
$B\geq 10$K, and [3.5--4.1$\times$]{#S6.SS4.p2.1.3 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_bold} faster than [torch.compile]{#S6.SS4.p2.1.4 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_typewriter}. At $B{=}1$K both [TileMaxSim]{#S6.SS4.p2.1.5
.ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} and PLAID are launch-bound below 0.1 ms
(PLAID is marginally faster), so the advantage of avoiding
$\mathbf{S}$-materialization only emerges once the kernel becomes
bandwidth-bound. The speedup over PLAID is smaller than over our naive
PyTorch baseline because PLAID's batched [bmm]{#S6.SS4.p2.1.6 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_typewriter} is itself a well-tuned cuBLAS call;
[TileMaxSim]{#S6.SS4.p2.1.7 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}'s gain comes
specifically from never writing $\mathbf{S}$ to HBM. Notably,
[TileMaxSim]{#S6.SS4.p2.1.8 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} is also *more
numerically faithful*: it accumulates dot products in FP32, whereas
PLAID's FP16 [bmm]{#S6.SS4.p2.1.10 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}
accumulates in lower precision, so on unnormalized inputs PLAID deviates
more from the FP32 reference than [TileMaxSim]{#S6.SS4.p2.1.11 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_smallcaps} does.
:::
:::::

sub-quantizers, <span class="math inline"><em>K</em> = 256</span>

::: {#S6.SS5.p1 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [5]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T5 "In 6.5. PQ Scoring Performance ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
shows [TileMaxSim-PQ]{#S6.SS5.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}
performance. The lookup-table approach achieves 1.9--2.9$\times$ speedup
over decompress-then-score for moderate batch sizes, and scales to 100K
documents where the baseline runs out of memory. The speedup is lower
than for full-precision MaxSim because PQ scoring is less memory-bound
(lower AI due to smaller data per document).
:::
::::

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table"><span id="S6.T6.4"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Table 6</span>. </span><span
id="S6.T6.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Achieved HBM
bandwidth utilization (% of 3.35 TB/s peak). <span
class="math inline"><em>N</em><sub><em>q</em></sub> = 32</span>, <span
class="math inline"><em>N</em><sub><em>d</em></sub> = 128</span>, <span
class="math inline"><em>d</em> = 128</span>. V2-MQ uses <span
class="math inline"><code>BQ</code> = 32</span> (single-pass). Measured
with <span id="S6.T6.5.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">torch.cuda.Event</span> (50
runs).</span></figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S6.SS6.p1 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [6]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T6 "In 6.6. Memory Bandwidth Utilization ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
shows that V2-MQ with $\texttt{BQ}{=}32$ (single-pass over all query
tokens) achieves up to [80.2%]{#S6.SS6.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}
of the H100's peak HBM bandwidth (2,687 GB/s of 3,350 GB/s) at
$B{=}100$K. This represents a 4.5$\times$ improvement over the prior
$\texttt{BQ}{=}16$ configuration (which achieved only 17.6%) because
setting $\texttt{BQ}{=}N_{q}$ eliminates all redundant document
re-reads: each document embedding is loaded from HBM *exactly once*. The
bandwidth utilization scales with batch size due to amortization of the
fixed query-loading cost and improved memory coalescing at larger access
granularities. The remaining 20% gap to peak bandwidth is attributable
to: (1) register pressure from maintaining $N_{q}{=}32$ running maxima
simultaneously, (2) non-power-of-two dimensions limiting vectorized
loads, and (3) atomic accumulation overhead. Our 80% bandwidth
utilization is high for a memory-bound kernel. For context,
FlashAttention-3 (a compute-bound kernel) reaches
$\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}75% of peak *compute*, a different roofline
regime. Our design is near-optimal for the memory-bound MaxSim workload
on this hardware.
:::
::::

::::::: {#S6.SS7 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [6.7. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Roofline Analysis and Detailed Profiling {#roofline-analysis-and-detailed-profiling .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S6.SS7.p1 .ltx_para}
All MaxSim variants are deeply memory-bound on the H100, with arithmetic
intensities of 14--32 FLOP/byte versus the 591 FLOP/byte crossover
point. This confirms that *reducing memory traffic, not increasing
compute throughput, is the correct optimization strategy*, validating
our IO-aware kernel design approach.
:::

::: {#S6.SS7.p2 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [7]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T7 "In 6.7. Roofline Analysis and Detailed Profiling ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
presents detailed profiling results from CUDA-event measurements. V2-MQ
with $\texttt{BQ}{=}32$ achieves 2,660 GB/s (79.4% of H100's 3.35 TB/s
peak) at $B{=}100$K, with corresponding achieved compute of
85.1 TFLOP/s. The achieved compute is only 4.3% of peak FP16 Tensor Core
throughput (1979 TFLOP/s), confirming that the kernel is entirely
memory-bound. The high bandwidth utilization validates our single-pass
design: reading each document embedding exactly once from HBM.
:::

class="math inline"><code>BQ</code> = 32</span>). <span
class="math inline"><em>N</em><sub><em>q</em></sub> = 32</span>, <span
class="math inline"><em>N</em><sub><em>d</em></sub> = 128</span>, <span
class="math inline"><em>d</em> = 128</span>. All measurements via CUDA
events (50 runs, mean <span class="math inline">±</span> std). This is a
dedicated profiling run; the few-percent throughput differences across
tables at <span class="math inline"><em>B</em> = 10</span>K
(57–60M docs/s; 58.8M on real MS MARCO embeddings) reflect run-to-run
variance and synthetic-vs-real data, not a different
configuration.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S6.SS7.p3 .ltx_para}
For PQ scoring via [TileMaxSim-PQ]{#S6.SS7.p3.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_smallcaps}, the arithmetic intensity is even lower (5--31
FLOP/byte depending on batch size) because each document token is
represented by only $M$ bytes of codes. This makes the lookup-table
approach particularly effective: by replacing HBM reads of decompressed
vectors with shared-memory table lookups, we convert memory-bound I/O
into fast SRAM accesses.
:::

::: {#S6.SS7.p4 .ltx_para}
V2-MQ operates at 32 FLOP/byte, yielding a theoretical peak of
$32\times 3.35=107$ TFLOP/s on the memory-bound side of the roofline.
Our achieved throughput of 85.1 TFLOP/s represents
[79.5%]{#S6.SS7.p4.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} of this memory-bound
ceiling, confirming near-optimal utilization of available HBM bandwidth.
The remaining gap stems from: (1) register pressure from $N_{q}{=}32$
simultaneous running maxima reducing SM occupancy, (2) non-power-of-two
dimensions limiting vectorized loads, and (3) atomic accumulation for
the final score output.
:::
:::::::

::::::::::::::::::: {#S6.SS8 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [6.8. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Scaling Analysis {#scaling-analysis .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S6.SS8.p1 .ltx_para}
We conduct a comprehensive scaling analysis across five dimensions to
characterize [TileMaxSim]{#S6.SS8.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_smallcaps}'s behavior.
:::

:::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px1 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Batch Scaling (1K to 500K documents). {#batch-scaling-1k-to-500k-documents. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px1.p1 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [8]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T8 "In Batch Scaling (1K to 500K documents). ‣ 6.8. Scaling Analysis ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
shows that V2-MQ with $\texttt{BQ}{=}32$ achieves high bandwidth
utilization that scales with batch size: from 17.5% at $B{=}1$K to
[81.5%]{#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} at $B{=}200$K.
Throughput plateaus at $\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}83M docs/s for
$B\geq 100$K, representing near-optimal utilization of HBM bandwidth. We
verified scaling up to 500K documents (16.4 GB of embeddings) on a
single H100.
:::

:::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px2 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Embedding Dimension Scaling. {#embedding-dimension-scaling. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px2.p1 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [9]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T9 "In Embedding Dimension Scaling. ‣ 6.8. Scaling Analysis ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
shows throughput as the embedding dimension $d$ varies from 64 to 768.
V2-MQ with dimension tiling achieves 90.5M docs/s at $d{=}64$ (relevant
for dimensionality-reduced ColBERT models), 60.9M at $d{=}128$ (standard
ColBERT), and 14.7M at $d{=}768$ (full BERT dimension). For $d>128$,
V2-MQ automatically switches to a dimension-tiled variant that tiles
over the embedding dimension in chunks of 128, accumulating partial dot
products before computing the max. Bandwidth utilization *increases*
with $d$ (from 44% at $d{=}64$ to 86% at $d{=}768$) because larger
embeddings provide better memory coalescing.
:::

:::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px3 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Query Token Scaling. {#query-token-scaling. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px3.p1 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [10]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T10 "In Query Token Scaling. ‣ 6.8. Scaling Analysis ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
shows throughput as $N_{q}$ varies from 8 to 64. With
$\texttt{BQ}{=}N_{q}$, V2-MQ reads each document embedding once
regardless of $N_{q}$, so throughput degrades gracefully: from 67.0M at
$N_{q}{=}8$ to 35.5M at $N_{q}{=}64$. The sublinear degradation occurs
because larger $N_{q}$ increases register pressure and the per-document
computation cost, but the dominant IO cost (reading D) remains constant.
:::

:::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px4 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Document Token Scaling. {#document-token-scaling. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px4.p1 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [11]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T11 "In Document Token Scaling. ‣ 6.8. Scaling Analysis ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
shows performance across $N_{d}\in\{32,64,128,256,512\}$. V2-MQ
maintains high throughput even for long documents ($N_{d}{=}512$: 22.0M
docs/s), and achieved bandwidth *increases* with $N_{d}$ (845 to
2,881 GB/s) because larger document tiles provide better memory
coalescing and amortize the per-tile overhead. Bandwidth peaks at 86% of
the H100's theoretical limit for $N_{d}{=}512$.
:::

::::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px5 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Kernel Profiling. {#kernel-profiling. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px5.p1 .ltx_para}
We profile [TileMaxSim]{#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px5.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_smallcaps} V2-MQ using both
[torch.profiler]{#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px5.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}
(CUPTI) and NVIDIA Nsight Systems ([nsys]{#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px5.p1.1.3
.ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}). At $B{=}100$K:
:::

::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px5.p2 .ltx_para}
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I3.i1}

  ::: {#S6.I3.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
  [CUDA time]{#S6.I3.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: The V2-MQ
  Triton kernel accounts for [99.7%]{#S6.I3.i1.p1.1.2 .ltx_text
  .ltx_font_bold} of total CUDA time. The only other GPU operations are
  score-buffer initialization ([fill\_]{#S6.I3.i1.p1.1.3 .ltx_text
  .ltx_font_typewriter} at 1.1 $\mu$s per call).
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I3.i2}

  ::: {#S6.I3.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
  [nsys breakdown]{#S6.I3.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: The
  kernel averages 589 $\mu$s per launch (5 averaged runs), with zero
  device-to-host memory copies during scoring.
  [cudaLaunchKernel]{#S6.I3.i2.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}
  overhead is 3.7 $\mu$s per call (0.6% of kernel time).
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I3.i3}

  ::: {#S6.I3.i3.p1 .ltx_para}
  [No multi-kernel overhead]{#S6.I3.i3.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}:
  Our fused design computes matmul + max-reduction + sum-reduction +
  atomic accumulation in a single kernel, eliminating the separate
  reduction pass needed by V1.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S6.I3.i4}

  ::: {#S6.I3.i4.p1 .ltx_para}
  [Occupancy]{#S6.I3.i4.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}: The V2-MQ
  kernel achieves $\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}50% theoretical occupancy
  (limited by register usage for $N_{q}{=}32$ running maxima). Despite
  this, the kernel is bandwidth-limited rather than occupancy-limited:
  increasing occupancy via [num_warps]{#S6.I3.i4.p1.1.2 .ltx_text
  .ltx_font_typewriter} tuning does not improve throughput, confirming
  memory bandwidth as the sole bottleneck.
  :::
:::
:::::

:::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px6 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### End-to-End Retrieval Latency. {#end-to-end-retrieval-latency. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px6.p1 .ltx_para}
We demonstrate [TileMaxSim]{#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px6.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_smallcaps} in a complete GPU-resident retrieval pipeline:
scoring a 500K-document corpus followed by
[torch.topk]{#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px6.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter} for
top-$k$ retrieval. The end-to-end latency is
[6.09 ms]{#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px6.p1.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} for 500K
documents (82.0M docs/s on synthetic data; 71.6M docs/s on real MS MARCO
passages), with top-$k$ selection adding $<$`<!-- -->`{=html}0.1 ms
overhead. At this rate, brute-force scoring of the full 8.8M MS MARCO
corpus would take $\sim$`<!-- -->`{=html}107 ms on a single H100,
competitive with approximate search methods that trade quality for
speed.
:::
::::

:::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px7 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Multi-GPU Scaling. {#multi-gpu-scaling. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS8.SSS0.Px7.p1 .ltx_para}
MaxSim scoring is embarrassingly parallel across documents: splitting
the candidate batch evenly across GPUs requires no inter-GPU
communication during scoring (only a final concatenation of per-shard
scores). Data-parallel sharding across two H100s therefore scales
near-linearly, since each GPU independently scores its half of the
candidates with the same per-shard throughput reported in
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [8]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T8 "In Batch Scaling (1K to 500K documents). ‣ 6.8. Scaling Analysis ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}.
:::
::::
:::::::::::::::::::

::::::: {#S6.SS9 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [6.9. ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Ablation Study {#ablation-study .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

:::: {#S6.SS9.SSS0.Px1 .section .ltx_paragraph}
##### Tile Size Sensitivity. {#tile-size-sensitivity. .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#S6.SS9.SSS0.Px1.p1 .ltx_para}
[[Table]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag} [12]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S6.T12 "In Tile Size Sensitivity. ‣ 6.9. Ablation Study ‣ 6. Evaluation ‣ TileMaxSim: IO-Aware GPU MaxSim Scoring with Dimension Tiling and Fused Product Quantization"){.ltx_ref}
shows the effect of tile sizes [BQ]{#S6.SS9.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_markedasmath .ltx_font_typewriter} (query tile) and
[BN]{#S6.SS9.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_markedasmath
.ltx_font_typewriter} (document tile) on V2-MQ throughput. Setting
$\texttt{BQ}{=}N_{q}{=}32$ is the single most impactful optimization: it
processes all query tokens in a single pass, reading each document
embedding from HBM *exactly once* instead of
$\lceil N_{q}/\texttt{BQ}\rceil$ times. The $\texttt{BQ}{=}32$
configuration achieves [4.5$\times$]{#S6.SS9.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.4 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_bold} higher bandwidth than $\texttt{BQ}{=}16$ because it
eliminates the redundant document re-read. Within the $\texttt{BQ}{=}32$
family, $\texttt{BN}{=}32$ is optimal as smaller tiles reduce register
pressure and increase SM occupancy. The Triton compiler's
[tl.dot]{#S6.SS9.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.5 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}
requires $\texttt{BQ}\geq 16$ for tensor core mapping.
:::
